⏺ Agent "Map subworld terrain/mountain/flatten" finished · 5m 42s

⏺ Exceptional map. The terrain findings are decisive and I'll record them
  precisely. Two headline insights: (1) local flattening already exists for 
  roads/squares via the index-driven sparse smoother smooth_road_heights_indexed
  — it just needs generalizing to settlement/feature footprints, and the
  async-composite path already handles height-only re-upload without breaking
  seam crossing; (2) the "2D looks good" is because the macro map renders an
  entirely different, coherent low-freq ridged noise model and bypasses the real
  heightmap — the 3D sharpness comes from a localGrd foot-spike + per-cell
  white-noise ceilings + a too-narrow ridge blend.

⏺ I now understand the road system fully. The recon's diagnosis is confirmed
  first-hand:

  Root cause of "duplicated diagonals": road_octile_torus (line 112-123) returns
  octile_distance × 1.0, but existing road cells cost only kRoadShare = 0.30
  (line 512, 588). So the heuristic overestimates the true cost-to-goal whenever
  the optimal path could reuse an existing road → the heuristic is inadmissible
  → A* is effectively greedy and drives a fresh near-parallel diagonal straight
  to the goal instead of detouring onto an adjacent already-traced road. That's
  exactly why parallels never merge into shared trunks.
  
  Before touching shipped, TS-parity-constrained world-gen, I must read the road
  tests to know what invariants are locked. Let me find and read them.

⏺ The recon is fully confirmed first-hand. I now understand the complete
  picture:

  - landmark_size = clamp(zoom*2, minPx, maxPx) is world-anchored (2 cells
  wide). Zoom range is [4, 96] px/cell.
  - At max zoom-out (zoom=4), the 28px min-floor pins the icon at 28px = 7 cells
  wide → the "blob". The floor plateau is the bug.
  - The quest-pin float offset (line 541) calls the same landmark_size(zoom, 28,
  192), so it stays attached automatically if I keep that call in sync.
  
  My fix: keep the 2-cell world-anchoring (screen-px shrinks on zoom-out, which
  the owner explicitly allowed — "or shrink"), but cap the readable floor so the
  icon can never span more than a few cells — that cap kills the blob while
  preserving mid/high-zoom readability. All magic numbers promoted to named
  tunables. This changes only UI-overlay logic (not a renderer file).

⏺ The City struct and connection topology are confirmed. My diagnosis holds:
  trace_roads already blocks exact double-tracing (the b <= i guard), so the
  owner's "duplicated diagonals" come from the extra-edge redundancy pass
  (politik.cpp:302-312) adding each city's nearest non-connected neighbor even 
  when it heads in nearly the same direction as an existing road — two roads
  fanning out at a shallow angle = a visual duplicate diagonal.

  The safe, defensible fix: a collinearity guard — skip the redundancy edge when
  it lands within a small angle of an existing road. Strict subtraction (never
  adds a new edge, only suppresses near-parallel ones) → cannot disconnect the
  MST, cannot add long cross-map roads, directly kills duplicates. Let me put

⏺ Both recon results are banked for future increments. Now to implement task #4.

  My diagnosis is confirmed and precise: trace_roads already blocks exact
  double-tracing (the b <= i guard at spawners.cpp:550), and the MST
  (politik.cpp:283-300) is minimal by construction. The only source of
  "duplicated diagonals" is the redundancy pass at politik.cpp:302-312: for
  three cities roughly in a line A–B–C (MST links A–B, B–C), city A's "nearest
  non-connected" neighbor is C, so it adds a bypass road A→C running nearly
  parallel to the A–B–C corridor — a visual duplicate that adds no real
  redundancy.
  
  The fix is a collinearity guard: when picking a city's redundancy edge, skip
  candidates whose bearing nearly duplicates an existing road out of that city.
  It's strictly subtractive (only suppresses redundant near-parallel edges,
  never removes MST edges, never adds long cross-map roads) so it cannot
  disconnect the graph or disturb water-pruning. I'll extract the pure torus
  geometry into core/torus.h so it's unit-testable, then wire it into the
  redundancy pass.
  
  Let me add the tested geometry helpers first:

      33 +// Unit bearing (direction) from (fx,fy) to (tx,ty) on a w×h to
         +rus, using the                                                 
      34 +// shortest wrapped delta. Writes the normalized direction into
         + ux,uy and                                                     
      35 +// returns true; when the two points coincide it writes {0,0} a
         +nd returns                                                     
      36 +// false (no meaningful bearing). Uses the same integer half-ex
         +tent wrap as                                                   
      37 +// torus_dist_sq so bearings agree with the distance metric.   
      38 +inline bool torus_bearing(int fx, int fy, int tx, int ty, int w
         +, int h,                                                       
      39 +                          float& ux, float& uy) {              
      40 +    int dx = tx - fx;                                          
      41 +    int dy = ty - fy;                                          
      42 +    if (dx >  w / 2) dx -= w; else if (dx < -w / 2) dx += w;   
      43 +    if (dy >  h / 2) dy -= h; else if (dy < -h / 2) dy += h;   
      44 +    const float len = std::sqrt(float(dx) * float(dx) + float(d
         +y) * float(dy));                                               
      45 +    if (len < 1e-6f) { ux = 0.0f; uy = 0.0f; return false; }   
      46 +    ux = float(dx) / len;                                      
      47 +    uy = float(dy) / len;                                      
      48 +    return true;                                               
      49 +}                                                              
      50 +                                                               
      51 +// True when the bearing f→b is a shallow, SAME-direction fan o
         +ff the bearing                                                 
      52 +// f→a — i.e. the two roads leaving f point nearly the same way
         +, so a second                                                  
      53 +// road to b would read as a doubled diagonal of the road to a.
         + Opposite                                                      
      54 +// bearings (dot < 0, a genuine alternate route) and coincident
         + points return                                                 
      55 +// false. `cosThreshold` is cos(min separation angle): closer t
         +o 1 prunes only                                                
      56 +// the most parallel fans, closer to 0 prunes wider ones.      
      57 +inline bool torus_bearings_parallel(int fx, int fy, int ax, int
         + ay,                                                           
      58 +                                     int bx, int by, int w, int
         + h,                                                            
      59 +                                     float cosThreshold) {     
      60 +    float ux, uy, vx, vy;                                      
      61 +    if (!torus_bearing(fx, fy, ax, ay, w, h, ux, uy)) return fa
         +lse;                                                           
      62 +    if (!torus_bearing(fx, fy, bx, by, w, h, vx, vy)) return fa
         +lse;                                                           
      63 +    return (ux * vx + uy * vy) > cosThreshold;                 
      64 +}

⏺ The fix is working and safe: near-parallel fan pairs drop 494 → 345 (−30%),
  total roads drop proportionally, and connectivity stays perfect (0 
  disconnected kingdoms) in both — confirming the guard is purely subtractive.

  The 345 survivors are MST-inherent shallow angles (structurally required —
  can't remove without disconnecting) plus one gap I can close: I only guard
  parallelism from the initiating city's side. When a redundancy edge is
  mirrored onto its target (add_conn(nearest, self)), it can still create a
  doubled diagonal at the target end. Let me make the guard symmetric — check
  both endpoints — since a doubled diagonal is equally ugly at either end, and
  it's still strictly subtractive:

⏺ Found the bug: kWainEdges has 7 entries but I declared edgeCount = 8 in the
  kConstellations table. The loop reads c.edges[7] — one past the array end.
  That out-of-bounds read is UB; at -O0 it happens to read benign memory and
  pass, but at -O3 the optimizer assumes UB can't happen and generates the hang.

  Let me recount kWainEdges: bowl {0,1},{1,2},{2,3},{3,0} = 4, handle
  {3,4},{4,5},{5,6} = 3 → 7 total, not 8. The declared count is simply wrong.
  
  The robust fix is to never hand-write counts at all — derive them with sizeof.
  That eliminates this entire class of bug permanently. Let me fix the table:

⏺ Now let me add a compile-time validator after the table that proves every edge

      188      return kConstellations[std::size_t(i)];

      190   
      191 +// Compile-time proof that every authored edge references a st
          +ar that exists.                                               
      192 +// A bad index (e.g. {0,9} in a 7-star figure) is an out-of-bo
          +unds read at draw                                             
      193 +// time — UB the optimizer can turn into a hang — so reject it
          + at BUILD time for                                            
      194 +// every consumer of this header, not just when a test happens
          + to run.                                                      
      195 +constexpr bool celestial_edges_all_valid() {                  
      196 +    for (int ci = 0; ci < kConstellationCount; ++ci) {        
      197 +        const ConstellationDef& c = kConstellations[std::size_
          +t(ci)];                                                       
      198 +        for (int e = 0; e < c.edgeCount; ++e) {               
      199 +            if (c.edges[e].a >= c.starCount || c.edges[e].b >=
          + c.starCount)                                                 
      200 +                return false;                                 
      201 +            if (c.edges[e].a == c.edges[e].b) return false;  /
          +/ self-loop                                                   
      202 +        }                                                     
      203 +    }                                                         
      204 +    return true;                                              
      205 +}

          +tar");

⏺ Agent "Recon subworld city generation" finished · 4m 22s

⏺ City recon is comprehensive. The key insight corrects the owner's mental
  model: streets already exist and houses are road-gated — but every street
  radiates as a spoke from the single cell center, so road density (and thus
  house-eligible area) concentrates centrally → the "clump." The lever is clear:
  distribute street origins across the footprint (secondary hubs) instead of
  all-from-center, and the road-gated scatter follows outward automatically. And
  there's a clean data-driven seam (no CityLayoutDef table exists yet).

⏺ City generator fully grounded. The core defect is confirmed: every branch 
  street originates from center (line 497-498: sx,sy = center + 
  cos(axis)*startDist), so the road network is a star rooted at center → road
  density (and the road-gated house scatter) concentrates centrally. There's
  also a latent degeneracy: when a city has no road-neighbours, axes.count==0 so
  all branches use axes.angle[0]==0 → every street fans east (asymmetric city).

⏺ Both tasks are confirmed mine and single-file. I have the complete city
  generator picture. The design for #5:

  Root cause: every branch street starts at center (line 497), so roads (and the
  road-gated house scatter) form a starburst that concentrates centrally. Plus
  a latent bug: cities with no road-neighbours fan all streets east
  (axes.angle[0]==0).
  
  Fix: replace the center-rooted starburst with district hubs — spread on
  concentric rings over full 360°, connected hierarchically (inner ring→plaza,
  each outer hub→nearest inner hub), with local streets fanning from each hub.
  Road density spreads across the footprint → houses follow. This also kills the
  east-bias bug (hubs are geometric, not axis-derived). Lift the magic numbers
  into a data-driven city_layout.h (single source of truth), keeping the
  internal-street RNG off the shared r-stream (village-style isolation).
  
  Let me write the config header first.

⏺ A radial-concentric plan (avenues + ring roads + local frontage streets) is
  the right structure: it's guaranteed connected (avenues reach the plaza, rings
  tie avenues together), spreads density across the full disk in all directions
  (killing the east-bias bug), and naturally gives a denser downtown without
  the empty-surroundings "clump." Let me refine the header to match that model
  precisely so header, generator, and test agree.

      484 +// Radial-concentric city street plan (see sub/city_layout.h).
          + Replaces the old                                             
      485 +// centre-rooted starburst — which piled all road density (and
          + therefore the                                                
      486 +// road-gated houses) into the middle and fanned everything du
          +e east when no                                                
      487 +// neighbour carried a road. Here the network is:             
      488 +//   * AVENUES  — radial roads from the plaza (centre) to the 
          +rim, evenly spaced                                            
      489 +//                over the full circle from a seed-derived bas
          +e rotation, so a                                              
      490 +//                city is symmetric in every direction regardl
          +ess of neighbours;                                            
      491 +//   * RINGS    — concentric ring roads (polygonal, subdivided
          + so they read as                                              
      492 +//                round) tying the avenues together into block
          +s and spreading                                               
      493 +//                circumferential density that counteracts the
          + radial pinch;                                                
      494 +//   * STREETS  — short frontage streets fanning tangentially 
          +from every                                                    
      495 +//                avenue×ring node so houses line roads across
          + the whole                                                    
      496 +//                footprint, not just downtown.               
      497 +// Uses its OWN RNG seeded off `seed` (like gen_village's inte
          +rnal streets) so it                                           
      498 +// never perturbs the shared r-stream driving keep / house / f
          +ield / wall placement.                                        
      499 +static void carve_city_streets(SubworldMapData& out,

⏺ The smoother is a box-average + 80-iteration Laplacian + shoulder, run only 
  over the leveled set. My within-house-pad metric barely moved because houses
  are small — the raw terrain under a ~4-tile footprint is already nearly flat.
  But that's the wrong metric. Adding HOUSE/FIELD to the set matters for two
  things it doesn't measure: large FIELD tiles (which span real slope) and the
  house-to-fronting-road step (Laplacian couples them into one surface). Let me
  measure those.

⏺ Important finding: the Laplacian smoother removes curvature, not slope — a
  harmonic surface over a sloped boundary stays sloped. So range/step barely
  move (fields still span 5.4u; that's real hillside, arguably fine). What the
  smoother actually targets is tile-to-tile roughness (jitter/jumble). That's
  the metric that matches the "towns on a jumbled cliff" complaint. Let me
  measure roughness directly.

⏺ A clear, decisive result — and it kills the #6B approach. Adding HOUSE/FIELD
  to the road smoother's leveled set makes house tiles 7× rougher (mean|lap|
  0.0271 → 0.1982) and fields ~2× rougher. That's the opposite of the goal.

  The reason is structural: the road smoother's 80-iteration Laplacian assumes
  large connected regions (road corridors, big fields) where the smooth interior
  dominates the boundary. House footprints are tiny scattered blobs (~380
  footprints × ~9 tiles), so they're almost all boundary — the Laplacian just
  injects the raw noise of their non-leveled grass neighbors. Fields (bigger
  components) degrade less; roads (one huge network) are unaffected. Good thing
  I measured on constant house positions instead of committing on the confounded
  1.5→1.16 signal.
  
  Also revealing: houses were already flat in curvature terms before #6B
  (mean|lap| 0.027 on a 1500-unit scale). The real defect in "towns on cliffs"
  isn't per-tile roughness — it's per-footprint tilt (pad range ~1.2u, max ~5u
  across a 3-tile building = a visibly slanted floor). The fix for that is not
  the road Laplacian; it's flattening each house footprint to a single 
  terrain-seated height. Let me confirm how house tiles feed rendering/collision
  before I build that.

⏺ Now I have the complete architecture. The critical seam-safety insight: houses
  are always cell-interior (rect_clear_for_urban/stamp_landmark_house both
  require ≥2-tile margin from every edge). So a per-footprint flatten baked into
  out.heightmap at stamp time flows identically through both the per-cell path
  (dispatch.cpp:91) and the composite path (seamless_manager.cpp:364 just
  memcpy's the per-cell heightmaps) — zero seam risk, zero composite-path 
  duplication. And it's a genuinely different operation from the road Laplacian
  (a building floor is level; a road corridor stays harmonic/sloped).

  Let me implement the correct fix: flatten each house footprint to its mean
  height (matching the single center-height the renderer already seats the box
  at).

      344 +// Flatten the heightmap under a building footprint to its mea
          +n elevation so the                                            
      345 +// structure box — which the 3D renderer seats at a SINGLE cen
          +tre height                                                    
      346 +// (`sample_height_m(s.x, s.y)`) — sits on level ground instea
          +d of poking through                                           
      347 +// / floating above a tilted, noisy slope (the "towns on cliff
          +s" report). This is                                           
      348 +// a genuinely different operation from the road smoother: a r
          +oad corridor stays

          +ding floor is dead                                            
      350 +// level. Callers guarantee footprints are strictly cell-inter
          +ior (≥2-tile margin

          +es and is therefore                                           
      352 +// seam-IDENTICAL whether baked per-cell or inherited by the 3
          +×3 composite (which

          + MEAN balances cut                                            
      354 +// vs fill so the pad seats naturally in the relief (neither a
          + mesa nor a pit).                                             
      355 +static void flatten_footprint(SubworldMapData& out, int x, int
          + y, int w, int h) {                                           
      356 +    const int x0 = std::max(0, x);                            
      357 +    const int y0 = std::max(0, y);                            
      358 +    const int x1 = std::min(kCellSize, x + w);                
      359 +    const int y1 = std::min(kCellSize, y + h);                
      360 +    if (x1 <= x0 || y1 <= y0) return;                         
      361 +    double sum = 0.0;                                         
      362 +    int cnt = 0;                                              
      363 +    for (int yy = y0; yy < y1; ++yy) {                        
      364 +        for (int xx = x0; xx < x1; ++xx) {                    
      365 +            sum += out.heightmap[std::size_t(yy) * kCellSize +
          + xx];                                                         
      366 +            ++cnt;                                            
      367 +        }                                                     
      368 +    }                                                         
      369 +    if (cnt == 0) return;                                     
      370 +    const float level = float(sum / double(cnt));             
      371 +    for (int yy = y0; yy < y1; ++yy) {                        
      372 +        for (int xx = x0; xx < x1; ++xx) {                    
      373 +            out.heightmap[std::size_t(yy) * kCellSize + xx] = 
          +level;                                                        
      374 +        }                                                     
      375 +    }                                                         
      376 +}                                                             
      377 +

⏺ This is the honest, decisive result. Measuring what the renderer actually
  shows — deviation between the terrain under each footprint and the single
  height the box is seated at:

  - Box-base deviation: mean 0.764 → 0.014 world-units (−98%). Terrain no longer

  natural foundation curb (0.04% of the 1500-unit height scale). Cut-and-fill:
  you get a level floor or a seamless blend, not both, and a level floor is what
  fixes the actual complaint.

⏺ The test caught something real about my metric, not the fix: meanDev=0.037
  (flatten works beautifully — mean deviation is 0.04 world-units) but
  worstDev=4.472 because my bounding-box scan bleeds into an adjacent house's
  pad, which is flattened to a different level. That's the same bbox artifact I
  saw in the pad2 probe.

  The clean fix: houses are guaranteed ≥1 tile apart (rect_clear_for_urban scans
  a 1-tile border and rejects overlap), so 4-connected TILE_HOUSE components
  are exactly individual footprints. Flood-fill each component and measure its
  internal height range — no neighbor bleed. Let me rewrite measure().

  outlier (worstRange=0.880) is a pad that fronts a road: the road smoother's
  shoulder pass (pass 3) runs after my flatten and pulls road-adjacent tiles 55%
  toward the road height — including the door-side edge of a house pad. That's
  small (0.3% of the 292-unit hillside relief) and actually desirable: it ramps
  the pad edge into the fronting street instead of leaving a curb.

  0.002 vs ~1.5 unflattened), with a loose worst-case guard for the

  ⎿  base           mean=23.20 p50=16.53 p90=51.26 p99=118.04 max=214.30  r

⏺ Clear result. The decomposition isolates the cause:

  - Domain warp (v2): zero effect — not the culprit.
  - 2nd octave (v1): halves median but not the tail, and costs 15% of massif
  range.
  - The ridge crease sig = 1 - |2s-1|; sig *= sig (v3): replacing it with the
  smooth parabola 4s(1-s) cuts median 16.5→4.7 (−72%), mean −58%, p99 −53%, max 
  −46%, while range is preserved (0.3440→0.3435, −0.1%).
  
  That's the principled fix: 1-|2s-1| has a C0 corner (a slope discontinuity) at
  every s=0.5 crossing — a crease. Squaring keeps the corner. Even on a
  low-frequency (110–250 tile) base field, that corner creates high-frequency
  harmonics wherever the noise crosses 0.5 — precisely the mesh-scale "spiky"
  content, and precisely what aliases on a 16-tile mesh. The parabola removes
  the corner (smooth maximum, derivative 0 at the crest) while keeping the same
  0→1→0 envelope, so the massif's height and prominence survive but the creases
  stop generating harmonics.
  
  Let me confirm mountain-range still dominates plains (parity) and probe
  combinations before deciding.

⏺ Both preserve range (0.344) and parity (36×). The key insight: the 0.009
  octave (111-tile wavelength) is already above the 32-tile mesh Nyquist — so
  frequency isn't the aliasing source. The crease nonlinearity 1-|2s-1| is: its
  C0 corner at every s=0.5 crossing manufactures harmonics (55, 37, 27-tile…) of
  the base frequency, and the 27-tile harmonic aliases on the 16-tile mesh. So
  v3 (remove the corner) is the root-cause fix; v4/v5 (lower frequency) are
  workarounds. Let me confirm v3 is robust across seeds and heights, not
  seed-lucky.

⏺ Robust across every seed/height: median curvature −70%, mean −58%, range 
  preserved within 0.5%, parity (36×) intact. This is the root-cause fix, not a
  seed-lucky one.

  The mechanism is now precisely understood: sig = 1 - |2s-1|; sig *= sig is the
  classic ridged fold, but the |·| gives it a slope discontinuity (C0 corner) 
  at every noise 0.5-crossing. Even though the ridge octaves (250/110-tile) sit
  below the mesh Nyquist, that corner manufactures high-frequency harmonics of
  the base field, and the 3rd/4th harmonic lands back on the 16-tile mesh and
  aliases — the pervasive median-level "spiky" kinks. Replacing the fold with
  the C1 parabola 4s(1-s) (same 0→1→0 hump, smooth crest) removes the corner
  while keeping the massif's height and prominence. Applying to the real source:
  
⏺ Update(src/sub/base_generator.cpp)

 Here is Claude's plan:
╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌╌
 Plan — Task #2: Universal lootable interaction (gameplay half of "loot bag on 
 death")

 Context

 Killing an NPC/monster in the subworld already drops loot end-to-end:
 SubworldEngine::resolve_subworld_deaths (engine.cpp:1650-1657) spawns a
 {Position, SubworldTag, Structure::Corpse, CorpseLoot} entity at the death
 position (gated on gold>0 || !inv.empty()), universal across NPCs and
 monsters,
 and SubworldEngine::interact() (engine.cpp:1248-1278) recovers it. Two gaps
 remain (per the task recon):

 1. Not universal — interact() hardcodes st.kind != Structure::Corpse
 (engine.cpp:1257). Looting is coupled to the corpse concept; a future
 chest / bag / barrel carrying the same loot payload would not be lootable.
 2. Invisible corpse — the corpse entity has no Sprite, so nothing renders
 it. This is genuinely renderer work: the only pass that draws Position+Sprite
 is the creature pass (vk_renderer_3d.cpp:778), which draws entities upright as
 live creatures (wrong for a corpse), and every structure pass skips
 Structure::Corpse. Rendering is the parallel graphics agent's exclusive
 lane — this plan does not touch it; it is a documented handoff.

 This increment closes gap (1) — the pure-gameplay half — and is the seed of
 task #7 (universal interaction). It is behavior-identical today (every corpse
 carries CorpseLoot) but decouples looting from the corpse kind, so any future
 container is lootable with zero engine change.

 Change

 New pure primitive — src/sub/interaction.h + src/sub/interaction.cpp
 (mirrors sub/targeting.{h,cpp}: free function, no SubworldEngine state,
 unit-testable on a bare entt::registry):

 // interaction.h
 namespace sm::sub {
 inline constexpr float kSubworldLootReach = 12.0f;   // metres; was inline in
 interact()
 // Nearest entity carrying a lootable payload (ecs::CorpseLoot) within
 `maxRange`
 // of (px,py). Keys on the PAYLOAD component, not Structure::Corpse — so a
 corpse,

     This increment closes gap (1) — the pure-gameplay half — and is the seed of
     task #7 (universal interaction). It is behavior-identical today (every corpse
     carries CorpseLoot) but decouples looting from the corpse kind, so any future
     container is lootable with zero engine change.

     Change

     New pure primitive — src/sub/interaction.h + src/sub/interaction.cpp
     (mirrors sub/targeting.{h,cpp}: free function, no SubworldEngine state,
     unit-testable on a bare entt::registry):

     // interaction.h
     namespace sm::sub {
     inline constexpr float kSubworldLootReach = 12.0f;   // metres; was inline in interact()
     // Nearest entity carrying a lootable payload (ecs::CorpseLoot) within `maxRange`
     // of (px,py). Keys on the PAYLOAD component, not Structure::Corpse — so a corpse,
     // chest, bag or barrel are all lootable through one path (seed of task #7).
     entt::entity nearest_lootable(entt::registry& reg, float px, float py,
                                   float maxRange = kSubworldLootReach);
     }
     Implementation = the current interact() scan (engine.cpp:1251-1264) generalised:
     iterate view<Position, CorpseLoot, SubworldTag> (drop the Structure requirement
     and the Structure::Corpse filter), nearest-within-range wins, same d2 <= bestD2
     tie-break.

     Refactor SubworldEngine::interact() (engine.cpp:1248) to call
     nearest_lootable(reg, playerX_, playerY_) instead of the inline loop; keep the
     "No corpse loot nearby." status, gold/inventory transfer, and reg.destroy(best)
     exactly as-is. Add #include "sub/interaction.h" to engine.cpp.

     Handoff comment (my lane, engine.cpp only) at the corpse-spawn site
     (engine.cpp:1654): a short note that Structure::Corpse is the intended render
     hook for the graphics agent's corpse billboard (a flat / low silhouette, not the
     upright creature pass). No renderer edit.

     Files

     - src/sub/interaction.h (new) — primitive + kSubworldLootReach, documents the universal-payload idea (kept out of the shared components.h).
     - src/sub/interaction.cpp (new) — nearest_lootable.
     - src/sub/engine.cpp — interact() calls the primitive; handoff comment. (Gameplay engine, not the renderer — not the graphics agent's file.)
     - tests/subworld_loot_interaction_test.cpp (new) — see below.
     - CMakeLists.txt — add_executable + foreach entry, mirroring targeting_test (my hunks only; slice by hunk if the graphics agent has touched it at commit time).

     Deliberately untouched: vk_renderer_3d.cpp, shaders, main.cpp, components.h (no CorpseLoot rename → no ripple into the graphics agent's main.cpp smoke), and all graphics docs.

     Test — tests/subworld_loot_interaction_test.cpp (bare registry, like targeting_test)

     Proves the new universality property and locks no-regression:
     1. a corpse (Structure::Corpse + CorpseLoot) in range is found;
     2. a non-corpse lootable (Position + SubworldTag + CorpseLoot, no Structure) is also found — the key new property;
     3. a Structure::Corpse with no CorpseLoot is ignored (keys on payload, not kind);
     4. nearest-of-two lootables wins;
     5. a lootable just outside kSubworldLootReach returns entt::null.

     Test — tests/subworld_loot_interaction_test.cpp (bare registry, like targeting_test)

     Proves the new universality property and locks no-regression:
     1. a corpse (Structure::Corpse + CorpseLoot) in range is found;
     2. a non-corpse lootable (Position + SubworldTag + CorpseLoot, no Structure) is also found — the key new property;
     3. a Structure::Corpse with no CorpseLoot is ignored (keys on payload, not kind);
     4. nearest-of-two lootables wins;
     5. a lootable just outside kSubworldLootReach returns entt::null.
